\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda^{\ast}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{\beta^{2}}=\|\beta^{-1}\lambda^{\ast}_{1}w((\lambda^{\ast}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}x))\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\|\bar{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\widetilde{v}_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}}|^{2}dx=\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\theta_{1,k}^{3}}{L^{2}_{1,k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}}|^{2}dx=\frac{\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{\beta^{2}},

therefore

\lambda^{\ast}_{1}=\lambda^{\ast}_{2}=1.

From (
5.17
), (
5.18
) ,(
5.19
) and (
5.20
), we have that

(\widetilde{u}_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}},\widetilde{v}_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}})\rightarrow(\sqrt{2}\beta^{-1}w,\beta^{-1}w)\ \ \text{in}\ H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

Moreover, as the limit function
(\sqrt{2}\beta^{-1}w,\beta^{-1}w)
is independent of the sequence that we choose, which implies that the convergence is true for the whole sequence. Therefore

\big(\widetilde{u}_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}},\widetilde{v}_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}}\big)=\big(L^{-1}_{1,k}u_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}}(\theta^{-1}_{1,k}(x)),L^{-1}_{2,k}v_{b_{1,k},b_{2,k}}(\theta^{-1}_{2,k}(x))\big)\rightarrow\big(\sqrt{2}\beta^{-1}w,\beta^{-1}w\big),

in
\ H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
as
b_{1,k},b_{2,k}\to 0
and
b_{1,k}\backsim b_{2,k}
.

∎

Next, we prove Theorem
1.5
.
In order to prove Theorem
1.5
, we first give following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\overline{b}_{1},\ \overline{b}_{2},\ \tau_{1},\ \tau_{2}>0.
There exists
\widetilde{\beta}=\widetilde{\beta}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2})>0
such that if
0<b_{1}\leq\overline{b}_{1}
,
0<b_{2}\leq\overline{b}_{2}
and
0<\beta<\widetilde{\beta}
, then

(i)
2R^{2}_{0}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)<R^{2}_{1}(\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2},\rho,\beta)
,

(ii) for any
d_{i}>0,\ c_{i}>0,i=1,2
such that
d^{2}_{i}+c^{2}_{i}=b_{1}^{i},\ i=1,2
, then
R^{2}_{0}(c_{1},c_{2},\rho,\beta)+R^{2}_{0}(d_{1},d_{2},\rho,\beta)\leq R^{2}_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.2
,
0<R_{0}=R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)<R_{1}=R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)
are the roots of

g(t,b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)=\frac{1}{2}t^{\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{1}{4}(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2}+\rho\mathcal{D}_{3})t^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}|\beta|\mathcal{D}_{4}=\varphi(t,b_{1},b_{2},\rho)-\frac{1}{2}|\beta|\mathcal{D}_{4}

and the existence of
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
is guaranteed by the condition

\displaystyle\beta\left(2b^{\frac{3}{2}}_{1}+b^{\frac{3}{2}}_{2}\right)C^{3}_{3,3}C^{2}_{3,4}\sqrt{\mu_{1}b_{1}+\mu_{2}b_{2}+\rho b^{\frac{1}{2}}_{1}b^{\frac{1}{2}}_{2}}<\frac{2\sqrt{6}}{3}.

(5.21)

Let
\overline{b}_{1},\ \overline{b}_{2},\ \tau_{1},\ \tau_{2}>0
and consider the range of
\beta>0
such that (
5.21
) is satisfied with
b_{1}=\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\ b_{2}=\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2}
. Taking the limit as
\beta\rightarrow 0^{+}
, by the continuity we have that
R_{0}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)\rightarrow 0
and
R_{1}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)
converge to the only positive root of
\varphi(t,\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho)
. Particularity, for every
\overline{b}_{1},\ \overline{b}_{2},\ \tau_{1},\ \tau_{2}>0
fixed , there exists
\widetilde{\beta}=\widetilde{\beta}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2})>0
such that

\displaystyle 2R^{2}_{0}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)<R^{2}_{1}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)\ \ \text{whenever}\ 0<\beta<\widetilde{\beta}.

(5.22)

Let
0<b_{1}\leq\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},

0<b_{2}\leq\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2}
and
0<\beta<\widetilde{\beta}
. Under the condition of (
5.21
), we have that

\partial_{t}g(t,b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)=\partial_{t}\varphi(t,b_{1},b_{2},\rho).

It is easy to check that
\varphi(t,b_{1},b_{2},\rho)
has a unique critical point on
(0,+\infty)
, which is a strict maximum point in
\overline{t}=\overline{t}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho)
with
0<R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho)<\overline{t}<R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho)
, therefore

\partial_{t}g(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)=\partial_{t}\varphi(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho)>0.

By the implicit function theorem, we know that
R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)
is a locally unique
C^{1}
function of
((b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta))
with

\frac{\partial_{t}R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial b_{1}}=-\frac{\partial_{b_{1}}g(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial_{t}g(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}>0,

\frac{\partial_{t}R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial b_{2}}=-\frac{\partial_{b_{2}}g(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial_{t}g(R_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}>0.

Similarly, we can proof that
R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)
is a locally unique
C^{1}
function of

((b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta))
with

\frac{\partial_{t}R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial b_{1}}=-\frac{\partial_{b_{1}}g(R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial_{t}g(R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}<0,

\frac{\partial_{t}R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial b_{2}}=-\frac{\partial_{b_{2}}g(R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}{\partial_{t}g(R_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta),b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)}<0.

So,
R_{0}
is monotone increasing in
b_{1},b_{2}
and
R_{1}
is monotone decreasing in
b_{1},b_{2}
. By (
5.22
) and the monotonicity of
R_{1}
, we have
2R^{2}_{0}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)<R^{2}_{1}(\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2},\rho,\beta)
. For any
d_{i}>0,\ c_{i}>0,i=1,2
such that
d^{2}_{i}+c^{2}_{i}=b_{1}^{i},\ i=1,2
, then

\displaystyle R^{2}_{0}(c_{1},c_{2},\rho,\beta)+R^{2}_{0}(d_{1},d_{2},\rho,\beta)<2R^{2}_{0}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta)<2R^{2}_{0}(\overline{b}_{1}+\tau_{1},\overline{b}_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)

\displaystyle<2R^{2}_{1}(\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2},\rho,\beta)<R^{2}_{1}(b_{1},b_{2},\rho,\beta).

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Suppose that there exists a
\epsilon_{0}>0
, a sequence of initial date
\{u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n}\}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and a sequence
\{t_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the solution
(u_{n},v_{n})
of (
1.14
) with the initial date
(u_{n}(0,\cdot),v_{n}(0,\cdot))=(u_{n}(\cdot),v_{n}(\cdot))
satisfies

dist_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}((u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n}),\mathcal{M}_{b_{1},b_{2}})<\frac{1}{n}

and

dist_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}((u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)),\mathcal{M}_{b_{1},b_{2}})\geq\epsilon_{0}.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\{(u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n})\}\subset\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}},
since

dist_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}((u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n}),\mathcal{M}_{b_{1},b_{2}})\rightarrow 0\ \text{as}\ \ n\rightarrow+\infty.

So,
\|u^{0}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}:=b_{1n}\rightarrow b_{1}

\|v^{0}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}:=b_{2n}\rightarrow b_{2}
and
J_{\beta}(u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n})\rightarrow m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})
. By (i) of Lemma
5.3
and the continuity, we can deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(|\nabla u^{0}_{n}|^{2}+|\nabla v^{0}_{n}|^{2})dx<R_{0}(b_{1}+\tau_{1},b_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)<R_{1}(b_{1n}+\tau_{1},b_{2n}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)

for every
n
large enough. Since

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(|\nabla u^{0}_{n}|^{2}+|\nabla v^{0}_{n}|^{2})dx\in[R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta),R_{1}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)],

we have
J_{\beta}(u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n})\geq 0
. Thus, we can deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(|\nabla u^{0}_{n}|^{2}+|\nabla v^{0}_{n}|^{2})dx<R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)<R_{0}(b_{1}+\tau_{1},b_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta).

Since
(u^{0}_{n},v^{0}_{n})\in A_{R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)}
, if
(u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)
exist from
A_{R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)}
there exists
t_{n}\in(0,T_{\max})
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(|\nabla u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)|^{2}+|\nabla v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)|^{2})dx=R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta);

however,
J_{\beta}(u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)\geq h(R_{0})=0
contract with the conservation of energy. Therefore, the solutions starting in
A_{R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)}
are globally defined in time and satisfy

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(|\nabla u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)|^{2}+|\nabla v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)|^{2})dx<R_{0}(b_{1n},b_{2n},\rho,\beta)<R_{0}(b_{1}+\tau_{1},b_{2}+\tau_{2},\rho,\beta)

for every
t_{n}\in(0,+\infty)
.
Then by the conservation laws of energy and mass

\{(u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot))\}

is a minimizing sequence. Thus,
\{(u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot))\}
is a minimizing sequence of
m^{r}_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})
.
Then according the proof of Theorem
1.3
there exists
(u_{0},v_{0})\in\mathcal{M}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
such that
(u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot))\rightarrow(u_{0},v_{0})
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which contradicts to

dist_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}((u_{n}(t_{n},\cdot),v_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)),\mathcal{M}_{b_{1},b_{2}})\geq\epsilon_{0}.

∎

6
Proof of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7

In this section, we first give some preliminaries. For
N=4
, we have the Sobolev inequality

\mathcal{S}\|u\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2},\quad\forall u\ \in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}),

where
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
is the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
with respect to the norm
||u||_{D^{1,2}}:=\|\nabla u\|_{2}
. For
2<p<4
, the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality (see
[
39
]
) is

\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\leq C_{4,p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{\gamma_{p}}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{1-\gamma_{p}},\quad\forall\ u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4}),

(6.1)

where
C_{4,p}>0
is a constant and
\gamma_{p}=\frac{2(p-2)}{p}
.
If
p=4
,
\gamma_{4}=1
, then
\mathcal{S}=|C_{4,p}|^{-2}
.

We have the following already known results.

Lemma 6.1
.

(
[
28
, Corollary B.1]
)
Suppose
(u,v)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
(
N\geq 3
) is a nonnegative solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), then
(u,v)
is a smooth solution.

Lemma 6.2
.

(
[
28
, Lemma 2.3]
)
Suppose
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\beta>0
and
(u,v)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
is a nonnegative solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), then
u>0
implies
\lambda_{1}>0
;
v>0
implies
\lambda_{2}>0
.

From
[
12
,
38
]
, we get the least energy solutions to (
1.16
).
Note that (
1.16
) has semi-trivial solutions
(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{1}U_{\varepsilon},0)
and
(0,\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{2}U_{\varepsilon})
. Here, we are only interested in nontrivial solutions of (
1.16
), this is
(\sqrt{k_{1}}U_{\varepsilon},\sqrt{k_{2}}U_{\varepsilon})
, where
U_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
1.15
),
k_{1}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{2}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
and
k_{2}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{1}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
. The main results in this aspect are summarized below. Next, we turn to the related limiting elliptic system (
1.16
).

Lemma 6.3
.

(
[
12
, Theorems 1.5 and 4.1]
) For
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0
,
if
0<\rho<\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
or
\rho>\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
, then any positive least energy solution
(u,v)
of (
1.16
) must be of the form

(u,v)=\big(\sqrt{k_{1}}U_{\varepsilon},\sqrt{k_{2}}U_{\varepsilon}\big).

Solutions to (
1.16
) correspond to critical points of the functional

I(u,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\frac{1}{2}\big(|\nabla u|^{2}+|\nabla v^{2}|\big)dx-\frac{1}{4}\big(\mu_{1}|u|^{4}+\mu_{2}|v|^{4}+2\rho|u|^{2}|v|^{2}\big)dx,\ \

u,v\in D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}).
From
[
38
, Lemma 2.1]
, if
0<\rho<\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
or
\rho>\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
,
(u_{0},v_{0})
is a least energy solution of (
1.16
), we can deduce that

I(u_{0},v_{0})=\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{S}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\rho}=\sqrt{k_{1}+k_{2}}\mathcal{S}.

We first study the case
\beta<0
,
\mu_{i}>0(i=1,2)
and
\rho>0
.

Proof of Theorem 1.11.
If
\beta<0
, let
(u,v)
be a constraint critical point of
J_{\beta}
on
\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
and
u,v>0
. Therefore,
(u,v)
solves (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) for some
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
By the Pohozaev identity
P_{b_{1},b_{2}}(u,v)=0
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\lambda_{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle=\frac{4-N}{4}\big(\mu_{1}\|u\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\rho\|uv\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{6-N}{4}\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|u|^{2}vdx

For
N=4,5
, then one of the
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
is negative. With out of generality, we assume
\lambda_{1}<0
. It follows from Lemma
6.1
that
(u,v)
is smooth, and is in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
; thus,
|\Delta u|,|\Delta v|\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
as well, and stand gradient estimates for the Poisson equation (see formula (3,15) in
[
14
]
) imply that
|\nabla u|,|\nabla v|\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Combining the fact that
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we get
u(x),v(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to\infty
. Thus, we have

-\Delta u=\big(-\lambda_{1}+\beta v+\mu_{1}u^{2}+\rho v^{2}\big)u\geq\frac{-\lambda_{1}}{2}u>0\ \

for
|x|>R_{0}
, with
R_{0}>0
large enough, and then
u
is superharmonic at infinity. From the Hadamard three spheres theorem
[
37
, Chapter 2]
, this implies that the function
m(r):=\min\limits_{|x|=r}u(x)
satisfies

m(r)\geq\frac{m(r_{1})\big(r^{2-N}-r_{2}^{2-N}\big)+m(r_{2})\big(r_{1}^{2-N}-r^{2-N}\big)}{r_{1}^{2-N}-r_{2}^{2-N}}\qquad\forall R_{0}<r_{1}<r<r_{2}.

Since
u
decays at infinity, we have that
m(r_{2})\to 0
as
r_{2}\to+\infty
, it is not difficult to see that
r\mapsto r^{N-2}m(r)
is monotone non-decreasing for
r>R_{0}
. Moreover,
m(r)>0
for every
r>0
because
u>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Thus,

m(r)\geq m(R_{0})R_{0}^{N-2}\cdot r^{2-N}\qquad\forall r>R_{0}.

If
N=4
, it follows from
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
that
u\in L^{\frac{N}{N-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. We deduce that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{L^{\frac{N}{N-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{\frac{N}{N-2}}\geq C\int_{R_{0}}^{+\infty}|m(r)|^{\frac{N}{N-2}}r^{N-1}dr\geq C\int_{R_{0}}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{r}dr=+\infty,\end{split}

with
C>0
. This is a contradiction. If
N=5
, the fact that
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
does not imply that
u\in L^{\frac{N}{N-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
or that
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
for some
p\in(0,\frac{N}{N-2}]
. But, imposing such condition as an assumption, we still reach a contradiction.

If
N\geq 4
, and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a radial function by
[
4
, Radial Lemma A.II]
, there exist
C>0
and
R_{1}>0
such that

|u(x)|\leq C|x|^{-\frac{N-1}{2}}\ \ \text{for}\ \ |x|\geq R_{1}.

If
\beta<0
, let
(u,v)
be a non-trivial radial solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
).
Similarly, we assume
\lambda_{1}<0
.
Setting
p(x)=-\beta v-\mu_{1}u^{2}-\rho v^{2}
, then

-\Delta u+p(x)u=-\lambda_{1}u.

(6.2)

Therefore, for
N\geq 4
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}|x||p(x)|\leq\lim_{n\to+\infty}\big[C|x|^{\frac{3-N}{2}}+C|x|^{2-N}\big]=0.

By Kato’s result
[
24
]
, i.e. Schrödinger operator
H=-\Delta+p(x)
has no positive eigenvalue with an
L^{2}
-eigenfunction if
p(x)=o(|x|^{-1})
, then (
6.2
) has no solution. We get (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) has no non-trivial radial solution for
\beta<0
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

If
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, for all
(u,v)\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
, there exists
t_{(u,v)}
such that
t_{(u,v)}\star(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
.
t_{(u,v)}
is the unique critical point of the function
\Psi_{u,v}
and is a strict maximum point at positive level.
Moreover:

(1)

\Psi_{u,v}^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
and
P_{b_{1},b_{2}}(u,v)<0
iff
t_{(u,v)}<0
.

(2)

\Psi_{u,v}
is strictly increasing in
(-\infty,t_{(u,v)})
.

(3)
The map
(u,v)\mapsto t_{(u,v)}\in\mathbb{R}
is of class
C^{1}
.

Proof.

We are therefore considering the defocusing nonlinearity mass-critical. We rewrite (
4.2
) as

\displaystyle\Psi_{u,v}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2s}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\big(|\nabla u|^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}-\beta|u|^{2}v\big)dx-\frac{e^{4s}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\big(\mu_{1}|u|^{4}+\mu_{2}|v|^{4}+2\rho|u|^{2}|v|^{2}\big)dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{2s}}{2}\Big[\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big(1-\frac{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{1}\big)+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big(1-\frac{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{2}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{4s}}{4}\Big(\mu_{1}\|u\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\rho\|uv\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\Big).

s\star(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
if and only if
\Psi^{\prime}_{u,v}(s)=0
, it’s easy to see that if
\big[\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big(1-\frac{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{1}\big)+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big(1-\frac{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{2}\big)\big]
is positive, then
\Psi_{u,v}(s)
has a unique critical point
t_{(u,v)}
, which is a strict maximum point at positive level. Therefore, under the condition of
0<b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, we know that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\big(|\nabla u|^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}-\beta|u|^{2}v\big)dx>0
. If
(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
, then
t_{(u,v)}
is a maximum point, we have that
\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{u,v}(t_{(u,v)})\leq 0
. We claim that
\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{u,v}(t_{(u,v)})<0
. By contradiction, this is
\Psi^{\prime}_{u,v}(0)=\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{u,v}(0)=0
, then necessarily
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\big(\mu_{1}|u|^{4}+\mu_{2}|v|^{4}+2\rho|u|^{2}|v|^{2}\big)dx=0
, which is not possible because
(u,v)\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
. Thus,
\Psi_{u,v}^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.4
shows that the map
(u,v)\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}\mapsto t_{(u,v)}\in\mathbb{R}
is of class
C^{1}
. Finally,
\Psi^{\prime}_{u,v}(s)<0
if and only if
s>t_{(u,v)}
, then
P_{b_{1},b_{2}}(u,v)=\Psi^{\prime}_{u,v}(0)<0
if and only if
t_{(u,v)}<0
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

If
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, then the set
\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
is a
C^{1}
-submanifold of codimension 1 in
\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
, and it is a
C^{1}
-submanifold of codimension 3 in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume
\mu_{i},b_{i}>0(i=1,2)
and
\rho,\beta>0
. Let
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, then

m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2}):=\inf_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}J_{\beta}(u,v)>0.\\

Proof.

If
(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
, then by Gagliardo-Nirenberg and the Sobolev inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}\|u\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\rho\|uv\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|u|^{2}vdx

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\rho}\big[\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big]^{2}+\frac{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{1}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\frac{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}{3}b_{2}\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}.

Moreover,
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, and
\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\neq 0
(since
(u,v)\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
), we get

\inf_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\geq C>0.

So

\displaystyle m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}J_{\beta}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}\frac{1}{4}\big[\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}-\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|u|^{2}vdx\big]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}\frac{1}{4}\big(1-\frac{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}b_{1}}{3}\big)\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\frac{1}{4}\big(1-\frac{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}b_{2}}{3}\big)\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle\geq C>0.

∎

From the above lemmas, then
J_{\beta}\big|_{\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}}
has a mountain pass geometry. We need an estimate from above on
m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})=\inf\limits_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}}J_{\beta}(u,v)
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\beta,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0
,
\rho\in\big(0,\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}\big)\cup\big(\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\},\infty\big)
. If
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, then

0<m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})<\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2},

where
k_{1}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{2}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
and
k_{2}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{1}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
.

Proof.

From (
1.15
),
U_{\varepsilon}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{2}+|x|^{2}}
, taking a radially decreasing cut-off function
\xi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
such that
\xi\equiv 1
in
B_{1}
,
\xi\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}\backslash B_{2}
, and let
W_{\varepsilon}(x)=\xi(x)U_{\varepsilon}(x)
. We have (see
[
23
, Lemma 7.1]
),

\|\nabla W_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\mathcal{S}^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\quad\quad\|W_{\varepsilon}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\mathcal{S}^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{4}),

(6.3)

and

\|W_{\varepsilon}\|^{3}_{L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=O(\varepsilon),\quad\quad\|W_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\ln\varepsilon|).

(6.4)

Setting

\big(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\big)=\Big(a_{1}\frac{W_{\varepsilon}}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}},a_{2}\frac{W_{\varepsilon}}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}\Big),

then
(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
. From Lemma
6.4
, there exists
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\tau_{\varepsilon}\star(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{P}_{b_{1},b_{2}}
,
this implies that,

m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})\leq J_{\beta}\big(\tau_{\varepsilon}\star(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\big)=\max_{t\in\mathbb{R}}J_{\beta}\big(t\star(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\big),

and

\displaystyle\big[\|\nabla\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}+\|\nabla\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}\big[\mu_{1}\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}+\mu_{2}\|\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}

(6.5)

\displaystyle+2\rho\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big]+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\beta|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}dx.

Then,

\displaystyle e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\big[\mu_{1}\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}+\mu_{2}\|\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}+2\rho\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big]

\displaystyle=\big[\|\nabla\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}+\|\nabla\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}\big]-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\beta|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}dx

\displaystyle\geq\big(1-\frac{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}b_{1}}{3}\big)\|\nabla\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\big(1-\frac{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}b_{2}}{3}\big)\|\nabla\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}.

Therefore, for
\varepsilon
small enough, we have

\displaystyle J_{\beta}\big(\tau_{\varepsilon}\star(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{2}\big(\|\nabla\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}+\|\nabla\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big)-\frac{e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}dx

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{4\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{4}\big(\mu_{1}\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}+\mu_{2}\|\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}+2\rho\|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s_{1},s_{2}>0}\frac{s^{2}_{1}+s^{2}_{2}}{2}\|\nabla W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}-\frac{\big(\mu_{1}s^{4}_{1}+\mu_{2}s^{4}_{2}+2\rho s^{2}_{1}s^{2}_{2}\big)}{4}\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}dx,

where
s_{1}=\frac{e^{\tau_{\varepsilon}}b_{1}}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}
and
s_{2}=\frac{e^{\tau_{\varepsilon}}b_{2}}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}
.
Define

f(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{s^{2}_{1}+s^{2}_{2}}{2}\|\nabla W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}-\frac{(\mu_{1}s^{4}_{1}+\mu_{2}s^{4}_{2}+2\rho s^{2}_{1}s^{2}_{2})}{4}\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{4}.

Using that, for all
0<s_{1},s_{2}
,

\max_{s_{1},s_{2}>0}f(s_{1},s_{2})\leq\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

Finally,

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|\overline{W}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\tau_{\varepsilon}}}{2}\frac{b^{2}_{1}b_{2}}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|^{3}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|W_{\varepsilon}|^{3}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C}{\|W_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|W_{\varepsilon}|^{3}dx

\displaystyle\geq C|\ln\varepsilon|^{-\frac{1}{2}},

hence, we deduce that

\max_{t\in\mathbb{R}}J_{\beta}\big(t\star(\overline{W}_{\varepsilon},\overline{V}_{\varepsilon})\big)<\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}.

∎

Now, we are ready to show that the infimum is attained by nontrivial positive radial functions.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mu_{i},b_{i}>0(i=1,2)
, and
\rho\in\big(0,\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}\big)\cup\big(\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\},\infty\big)
.
If
0<\beta b_{1}<\!\frac{3}{2|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
,
0<\beta b_{2}<\!\frac{3}{|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and

0<m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})<\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2},

where
k_{1}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{2}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
and
k_{2}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{1}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
, then
m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})
can be achieved by some function
(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})\in\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}
which is real valued, positive, radially symmetric and radially decreasing.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.12
, we can also find a radial Palais-Smale sequence for
J_{\beta}\big|_{\mathrm{T}_{b_{1}}\times\mathrm{T}_{b_{2}}}
at level
m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})
such that
P(u_{n},v_{n})\to 0
and
u^{-}_{n},v^{-}_{n}\to 0
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. The rest of proof is similar to that of Lemma 5.5 in
[
31
]
, and just needs a slight modification..
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\mu_{i},\beta>0(i=1,2)
, and
\rho\in\big(0,\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}\big)\cup\big(\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\},\infty\big)
.
If
0<b_{1}<\frac{3}{2\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
and
0<b_{2}<\frac{3}{\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}}
, for any ground state
(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})
of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), then

J_{\beta}(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})=m_{\beta}(b_{1},b_{2})\to\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}\ \ \text{as}\ \ (b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0).

Moreover, there exists
\varepsilon_{b_{1},b_{2}}>0
such that

\big(\sigma_{1}u_{b_{1},b_{2}}(\sigma_{1}x),\sigma_{1}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}(\sigma_{1}x)\big)\to(\sqrt{k_{1}}U_{\varepsilon_{0}},\sqrt{k_{2}}U_{\varepsilon_{0}})\ \ \text{in}\ D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\times D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}),

for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
as
(b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0)
up to a subsequence, where

k_{1}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{2}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
and
k_{2}=\frac{\rho-\mu_{1}}{\rho^{2}-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
.

Proof.

By
P_{b_{1},b_{2}}(u_{b_{1}},v_{b_{2}})=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}

\displaystyle>J_{\beta}(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})=\frac{1}{4}\big(\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}-\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}|^{2}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}dx\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\big(1-\frac{2\beta b_{1}}{3}|C_{4,3}|^{3}\big)\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\frac{1}{4}\big(1-\frac{\beta b_{2}}{3}|C_{4,3}|^{3}\big)\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})},

then
\{(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})\}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
. Using the Hölder and Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality again,

\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}|^{2}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}dx\leq\beta|C_{4,3}|^{3}\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{\frac{4}{3}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{\frac{2}{3}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}b^{2}_{1}b_{2}\to 0,

as
(b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0)
. Therefore, it follows from
P(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})=0
that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\mu_{1}\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\rho\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+o(1).

From (
1.17
), we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{k_{1}+k_{2}}\mathcal{S}\big(\mu_{1}\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\beta\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq||\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}.

Thus, we distinguish the two cases

\displaystyle\text{either}\ \ (i)\ \ ||\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\to 0\ \ \text{or}(ii)\ \ ||\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\to l>0.

We claim that
(i)
is impossible. Indeed, if
l=0
, by
\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}}}(0)<0
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle<2\big[\mu_{1}\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle\quad+2\rho\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big]+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\beta|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}|^{2}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}dx,

we obtain a contradiction

\displaystyle\big(1-\frac{2\beta b_{1}}{3}|C_{4,3}|^{3}\big)\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\big(1-\frac{\beta b_{2}}{3}|C_{4,3}|^{3}\big)\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

\displaystyle<\frac{2}{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\rho}}\big[\|\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\big]^{2}.

The claim is proved. Therefore,
l>0
, we obtain that
J_{\beta}(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})\to\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{4}\mathcal{S}^{2}
as
(b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0)
.

Thus, we know that

\displaystyle||\nabla u_{b_{1},b_{2}}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\nabla v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\to(k_{1}+k_{2})\mathcal{S}^{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{1}\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\mu_{2}\|v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+2\beta\|u_{b_{1},b_{2}}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\to(k_{1}+k_{2})\mathcal{S}^{2},

as
(b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0)
. It follows that, up to a subsequence,
\{(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})\}
is a minimizing sequence of the minimizing problem (
1.17
). From Lemma
6.8
,
(u_{b_{1},b_{2}},v_{b_{1},b_{2}})
is radially symmetric. By
[
47
, Theorem 1.41]
or
[
26
, Lemma 3.5]
, up to a subsequence, there exists
\sigma_{1}:=\sigma_{1}(a_{1},a_{2})
such that for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

\big(\sigma_{1}u_{b_{1},b_{2}}(\sigma_{1}x),\sigma_{1}v_{b_{1},b_{2}}(\sigma_{1}x)\big)\to\big(\sqrt{k_{1}}U_{\varepsilon_{0}},\sqrt{k_{2}}U_{\varepsilon_{0}}\big)

in
D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\times D^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, as
(b_{1},b_{2})\to(0,0)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem 1.5.

The proof is finished when we combine Lemma
6.8
and Lemma
6.9
.
∎
